- No `relationship` = 'Husband' with `sex` = 'Female'
- No `relationship` = 'Wife' with `sex` = 'Male'
- `education-num` must be numerically consistent with `education` level
- `hours-per-week` should be typical for the `occupation` (e.g., executives work more than part-time)
- No logically impossible age-education combinations (e.g., age 16 with doctorate)

**Matching sample moments**: For each categorical variable (education, occupation, etc.), ensure the synthetic marginal frequencies match the user-provided sample frequencies within ±2%. For continuous variables (age, capital-gain/loss), match means ± one standard error.

Generate data row by row, strictly respecting the topological order: first age, then its children, then their children, and finally Income last. Do not generate any variable before its parents.</Task>

[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'occupation'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'race'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'workclass'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('capital-gain', 'Income'), ('capital-loss', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income')]</Causal structure>

**Important note**: The graph is strictly acyclic. All edges flow forward in time. For instance, age (fixed at birth) influences education and marital-status, but not vice versa. Income is an outcome, never a cause. `race` is treated as an outcome of `marital-status` (reflecting social selection), not as an immutable demographic—this matches the user data's patterns.

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce highly accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the user-provided samples. Follow this generation procedure strictly, using sample moments from the user-provided data:

### Step 1: Root cause – `age`
- Sample `age` from a normal distribution with mean=38.5, sd=13.0, clipped to [17, 90].
- Ensure the synthetic mean matches the sample mean within ±0.5 years.

### Step 2: First-level children (conditioned on `age`)
- **`education`**: Use a multinomial logistic model fitted on the user data with `age` as the only predictor. Probabilities for `education` categories should match sample proportions within ±1%.
  - Example anchor: Age <20 → Most likely "Some-college" or "HS-grad"; Age 40-60 → Most likely "Bachelors" or "Some-college".
- **`marital-status`**: Use a multinomial logistic model with `age` as predictor. Match sample proportions within ±1%.
  - Example: Age <25 → strong "Never-married" bias; Age >60 → strong "Married-civ-spouse" bias.
- **`occupation`**: Use a multinomial logistic model with `age` as predictor. Ensure distribution matches sample.
- **`hours-per-week`**: Sample from a conditional distribution given `age`, modeled as a right-skewed normal (mean=40, sd=10) with age adjustments (e.g., younger people work fewer hours on average).

### Step 3: Second-level children
- **`education-num`**: Deterministic mapping from `education`:
  - "Preschool"→1, "1st-4th"→2, "5th-6th"→3, "7th-8th"→4, "9th"→5, "10th"→6, "11th"→7, "12th"→8, "HS-grad"→9, "Some-college"→10, "Assoc-acdm"→11, "Assoc-voc"→12, "Bachelors"→13, "Masters"→14, "Prof-school"→15, "Doctorate"→16.
- **`occupation`**: Update using a multinomial logistic model conditioned on both `age` and `education`. Example: High education + age 25-50 → strong "Prof-specialty" or "Exec-managerial"; Low education + any age → strong "Craft-repair" or "Other-service".
- **`race`**: Sample from a multinomial conditioned on `marital-status`. Match sample proportions within ±1%.
  - Example: "Married-civ-spouse" → slightly higher "White" probability than "Never-married".
- **`relationship`**: Determined by `marital-status`:
  - If "Married-civ-spouse" or "Married-spouse-absent" → sample from {"Husband", "Wife"} with probabilities proportional to sample (e.g., 50/50).
  - If "Married-AF-spouse" → sample from {"Husband", "Wife"}.
  - If "Divorced", "Separated", "Widowed" → sample from {"Not-in-family", "Unmarried", "Other-relative"} with sample proportions.
  - If "Never-married" → sample from {"Not-in-family", "Unmarried", "Own-child"}.

### Step 4: Further descendants
- **`sex`**: Strongly determined by `relationship`:
  - "Husband" → "Male" with 99.9% probability, "Female" with 0.1%.
  - "Wife" → "Female" with 99.9% probability, "Male" with 0.1%.
  - Other relationships → sample from sample proportions (e.g., "Not-in-family" → 60% "Male", 40% "Female").
- **`workclass`**: Use a multinomial logistic model conditioned on `sex`, `education`, and `occupation`. Match sample proportions.
  - Example: "Female" + "Prof-specialty" → higher "Private"; "Male" + "Self-emp-not-inc" → common for "Craft-repair".

### Step 5: Update `hours-per-week`
- Re-sample `hours-per-week` conditioned on `age` and `occupation` using a regression model from the user data.
  - Example: "Exec-managerial" → mean=45, sd=8; "Other-service" → mean=35, sd=12.

### Step 6: Capital gains and losses
- For each row, sample `capital-gain` and `capital-loss` independently:
  - Both are zero with 95% probability (match sample).
  - Non-zero values: Use a log-normal distribution, mean=4000, sd=10000, clipped to [1, 99999].
  - Ensure small correlation with `occupation` (e.g., "Exec-managerial" slightly higher positive gains).

### Step 7: Income (target)
- Use a logistic regression model with weights:
  - Predictors: `age` (positive), `education-num` (strong positive), `hours-per-week` (positive), `capital-gain` (very strong positive), `capital-loss` (negative).
  - Probability of ">50K" should be approximately 24% overall (match sample).
  - Example: Age=50, education-num=13, hours=50, capital-gain=0 → ~40% chance >50K; Age=30, education-num=9, hours=30, capital-gain=0 → ~10% chance.

### Hard constraints (enforce after generation)
- **No logical violations**: 
  - `relationship`="Husband" → `sex` must be "Male"
  - `relationship`="Wife" → `sex` must be "Female"
  - `age` < 18 → `relationship` must be "Own-child" or "Not-in-family"
  - `age` < 18 → `education` must be "Preschool" through "12th", and `education-num` <= 8
- **Consistency checks**:
  - `hours-per-week` must be integer between 1 and 99.
  - `education-num` must exactly match the deterministic mapping from `education`.
  - No duplicate rows (unique row identities).
- **Matching sample moments**:
  - For categorical variables (`education`, `marital-status`, `occupation`, `race`, `sex`, `workclass`, `income`): each category's proportion must be within ±1% of the user sample proportion.
  - For continuous variables (`age`, `capital-gain`, `capital-loss`, `hours-per-week`): synthetic mean must be within ±0.5 standard errors of the sample mean.

### Final validation
- After generating all rows, compute the KL divergence between synthetic and sample distributions for all variables. If any KL divergence > 0.02, regenerate that variable with adjusted conditional probabilities.
- Check pairwise correlations (e.g., `education` vs `income`). If the Pearson correlation is off by more than 0.05 from the sample, adjust the income model.

Generate data row by row, strictly respecting the topological order. Do not generate any variable before its parents. This procedure ensures causal consistency, statistical fidelity, and logical soundness.

Ensure strict adherence to the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above: generate each variable conditioned on its direct causes, using realistic conditional distributions derived from the data. Enforce logical and demographic consistency:

- **Age** is a root cause influencing education (older individuals more likely have completed higher education, but with diminishing returns after a certain age), marital-status (age increases likelihood of being married or divorced), occupation (older individuals more likely in managerial or professional roles, less likely in service or labor occupations), and hours-per-week (younger adults may work fewer hours, older adults may work more but with more variety).

- **Education** directly determines education-num (monotonic mapping: e.g., 'Preschool' → 1, 'Doctorate' → 16) and Income (higher education strongly increases likelihood of high Income, but moderated by occupation and hours). **Education also directly causes occupation** (higher education opens more professional occupations, while lower education limits to labor or service jobs)—this is critical to avoid implausible pairings like low education with high-skill occupation.

- **Workclass** (e.g., 'Private', 'Self-emp-inc', 'Government') is influenced by sex (with realistic but non-deterministic distributions) and also directly causes occupation (some occupations are more common in certain workclasses, e.g., 'Exec-managerial' in 'Self-emp-inc' or 'Private').

- **Native-country** influences both occupation (e.g., 'Farming-fishing' more common in countries like 'Philippines' or 'Mexico') and education (e.g., 'United-States' has a different educational distribution than 'Philippines' or 'India'—reflect actual demographic trends from the data).

- **Occupation** then influences hours-per-week (professionals often work 40-50 hours, part-time roles in sales or service may be <30) and Income (managerial/professional roles have higher Income, while labor roles have lower).

- **Marital-status** directly causes relationship (e.g., 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' or 'Wife'; 'Divorced' → 'Not-in-family' or 'Unmarried'; 'Never-married' → 'Own-child' or 'Not-in-family' — maintain plausible and mutually exclusive mappings).

- **Relationship** then causes sex ('Husband' → male, 'Wife' → female, 'Own-child' → any sex, but with realistic proportions).

- **Hours-per-week** directly affects Income (more hours increase likelihood of higher Income, but with diminishing returns beyond 50 hours).

- **Income** (binary >50K or <=50K) is the joint effect of education, occupation, and hours-per-week; do not generate Income that is implausibly high for low education or part-time hours.

- **Capital-gain** and **capital-loss** are rare and significant: capital-gain (range 0-99999) should only be non-zero when Income is high (e.g., >50K), and capital-loss (range 0-4356) should only be non-zero when capital-gain is non-zero (e.g., reflecting investments). Keep both within reasonable ranges given Income level.

- Avoid cycles, implausible combinations (e.g., child with 'Doctorate' education; 'Married-civ-spouse' relationship with 'Never-married' marital-status; high Income with occupation 'Farming-fishing' and low education), and ensure all causal chains are logically coherent. Pay close attention to the following:

- **Causal ordering:** Generate variables in topological order (e.g., age first, then education, marital-status, etc., with Income and capital-related features last).
- **Conditional dependencies:** For every edge (A, B), the value of B must be generated based on the actual value of A, mimicking statistical patterns from real data (e.g., higher education leads to higher Income for most occupations; older individuals are more likely to be married).

- **No cycles or contradictions:** The DAG is acyclic; do not introduce feedback loops. After generation, verify that all causal paths produce consistent and plausible records.

By following these steps, you will create synthetic data that maintains high fidelity to the underlying causal structure and real-world constraints, reducing artificial patterns and improving quality. </Task>""

<Causal structure>
Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week')]
</Causal structure>

<Task>
Generate synthetic data that accurately and convincingly reflects the given causal structure and is statistically consistent with the user-provided sample data.  
Key guidelines:  
1. **Topological generation**: Generate variables in the order: age → education → marital-status → (education-num, occupation) → (relationship, hours-per-week) → workclass → Income → (capital-gain, capital-loss) → sex. For each variable, strictly condition on its direct causes.  
2. **Realistic conditional distributions**: Use the sample data to approximate realistic distributions. For instance:  
   - Age influences education level (e.g., younger individuals more likely to be in school) and marital-status (older more likely married).  
   - Education determines education-num (ordinal mapping) and occupation (e.g., higher degrees linked to professional roles).  
   - Occupation and hours-per-week jointly affect Income (high occupation + high hours → higher Income).  
   - Income has a strong positive correlation with capital-gain and negative with capital-loss.  
3. **Logical coherence**: Ensure no impossible combinations. Examples include:  
   - A 16-year-old with a PhD ("age"→"education").  
   - "Never-married" with "Husband" as relationship.  
   - High Income with very low occupation/hours-per-week.  
4. **Simplicity over over-constraint**: Avoid linking features that may introduce unrealistic constraints (e.g., do not let marital-status directly cause race—keep it simple).  
5. **Validation check**: After generation, verify that all causal paths produce plausible records. If a generated record violates basic real-world expectations (e.g., a minor working 100 hours/week), discard and resample.

Your goal is to produce synthetic data where each feature’s value is clearly derived from its causes, minimizing statistical artifacts and improving overall data quality.
</Task>

   - Occupation influences hours-per-week (e.g., professionals may work more) and workclass.
   - Hours-per-week positively influences Income.
   - Income strongly influences capital-gain (positive) and capital-loss (negative).
   - Relationship determines sex (e.g., "Husband" → "Male", "Wife" → "Female") with consistency.
   - Sex influences workclass (use realistic distributions from sample).
3. **Logical coherence**: Enforce strict consistency rules:
   - Marital-status and relationship must align (e.g., "Never-married" → no "Husband"/"Wife").
   - Race values must be realistic given marital-status patterns.
   - Sex must be consistent with relationship (e.g., "Husband" implies "Male", "Wife" implies "Female").
   - Avoid contradictions like high Income with very low hours-per-week.
   - Keep all continuous variables in realistic ranges (hours-per-week 1-99, capital-gain/loss ≥ 0, education-num 1-16).
4. **Causal conditioning**: For every edge (A, B), generate B directly from A. Never generate from non-causal features—use only direct causes. Sample conditional distributions (e.g., P(Income | education, hours-per-week)) from the user-provided data to maintain realism.
5. **Validation**: After generation, check each record for internal consistency. Discard and resample any record with implausible combinations (e.g., minor with doctorate, never-married with "Husband").

Your goal is to produce synthetic data where each feature’s value is strictly derived from its direct causes, minimizing statistical noise and maximizing fidelity to real-world patterns.
</Task>

>>>>>>>>>>>>>>> Epoch: 1, batch: 7 <<<<<<<<<<<<<<<< 
Instruction: Based on the analysis of the existing prompt-score pairs, the structural issues that inflate scores include redundant dependencies, unrealistic causal flows, and missing constraints. The best-performing prompt (23.81%) had a simpler structure but missed some logical connections, while higher-scoring prompts added cycles or implausible constraints.

To lower the score further, I will:
1. **Remove cycles** (e.g., `workclass` → `occupation` and `occupation` → `workclass` in some prompts).
2. **Introduce direct, realistic causes** (e.g., `education` → `capital-gain` is unrealistic; `Income` → `capital-gain` is better).
3. **Add demographic plausibility** (e.g., `age` → `workclass` for retirement/entry-level work, `native-country` → `race`).
4. **Simplify guidance** to avoid over-constraining that leads to mismatched distributions.

Here is the optimized prompt:

---

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'workclass'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'race'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'native-country'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('capital-gain', 'capital-loss'), ('native-country', 'race'), ('workclass', 'occupation')]
</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that strictly adheres to the given Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG). Generate each variable conditioned solely on its direct causes. Use realistic conditional distributions derived from the provided samples. Avoid implausible combinations (e.g., a 14-year-old with a doctorate, a divorced person under age 18, or high capital-gain without high Income). Maintain logical coherence along all causal chains:

- **Age** is a root cause that influences education (younger ages limit education level), marital-status (younger ages less likely married), and workclass (younger individuals more likely in 'Private', older in 'Self-emp-inc' or 'Without-pay' for retirement).

- **Education** determines education-num (monotonic mapping) and occupation (professional vs. service jobs). No low education with high-skill occupation like 'Exec-managerial'.

- **Marital-status** determines relationship (e.g., 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband'/'Wife'; 'Never-married' → 'Own-child'/'Not-in-family') with realistic proportions.

- **Relationship** determines race (e.g., 'Own-child' may have different racial distribution than 'Husband') and sex ('Husband' → male, 'Wife' → female, 'Own-child' → any sex).

- **Sex** influences native-country (e.g., male/female proportions vary by country of origin).

- **Workclass** (e.g., 'Private', 'Self-emp-inc') is influenced by age (older more likely self-employed) and causes occupation (e.g., 'Self-emp-inc' → 'Exec-managerial', 'Private' → 'Craft-repair').

- **Occupation** affects hours-per-week (e.g., 'Sales' part-time vs. 'Exec-managerial' 50+ hours) and Income (non-managerial roles rarely have high Income).

- **Hours-per-week** and occupation jointly influence Income: low hours (<20) with low Income, high hours (>60) with high Income only for professional occupations.

- **Income** (binary >50K or <=50K)  
  → **Capital-gain** (range 0–99999) should be >5000 only when Income >50K; for Income <=50K, capital-gain should be 0 or small (<1000).  
  → **Capital-loss** (range 0–4356) should only appear when capital-gain >0 (reflecting investment volatility). Keep both rare (e.g., <5% of rows for non-zero values).

- **Native-country** causes race to reflect real demographic correlations (e.g., 'Mexico' → 'White' or 'Other'; 'Jamaica' → 'Black'). Avoid contradictions like 'Japan' → 'Black'.

- Ensure no cycles (strict DAG adherence), no negative values, and all generated records match the demographic distributions (e.g., age ≥ 18 for private employment, age ≥ 25 for married with children). Use conditional probabilities derived precisely from the sample data to avoid distributional shifts.

---
"<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'race'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'workclass'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('capital-gain', 'capital-loss'), ('workclass', 'occupation'), ('native-country', 'occupation')]
</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that dutifully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Ensure strict adherence to the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above. Your generation process must follow this deterministic sequence:

1.  **Marginal Root Nodes:** First, generate 'age' and 'native-country' from the marginal distributions observed in the data without conditioning on any other features.
2.  **Conditional Generation:** Generate each remaining variable strictly conditioned only on its direct causes as defined in the graph. For example:
    *   `education` must be conditioned on `age`.
    *   `marital-status` must be conditioned on `age`.
    *   `education-num` must be conditioned on `education`.
    *   `occupation` must be conditioned on `education`, `workclass`, and `native-country`.
    *   `relationship` must be conditioned on `marital-status`.
    *   `race` must be conditioned on `relationship`.
    *   `sex` must be conditioned on `relationship`.
    *   `workclass` must be conditioned on `sex`.
    *   `hours-per-week` must be conditioned on `occupation`.
    *   `Income` must be conditioned on `education` and `hours-per-week`.
    *   `capital-gain` must be conditioned on `Income`.
    *   `capital-loss` must be conditioned on `capital-gain`.

3.  **Rigorous Logical & Statistical Validation:** After generating each sample, perform a check for the following specific constraints. If a violation is found, **reject the sample and regenerate it** until all constraints are met.
    *   **Age vs. Education:** A person under 18 cannot have a 'Doctorate' or 'Prof-school'. A person under 14 cannot have 'Assoc-voc' or 'Bachelors'. A person under 6 cannot 'Some-college'.
    *   **Age vs. Marital Status:** A person under 16 cannot be 'Married-civ-spouse' or 'Divorced'. A person under 14 cannot be 'Separated'.
    *   **Marital Status vs. Relationship:** 'Married-civ-spouse' must pair only with 'Husband' or 'Wife'. 'Not-in-family' must pair with 'Unmarried', 'Divorced', 'Separated', or 'Widowed'. 'Own-child' must pair with 'Never-married'.
    *   **Sex vs. Workclass:** Gender roles in professions must be statistically grounded in the raw data. For example, if 'Female' in the data has a 95% probability of being 'Private' workclass, do not generate a 'Female' with 'Self-emp-inc' 30% of the time. Use the empirical conditional probabilities from the data.
    *   **Income Consistency:** A person with 'Income <=50K' **cannot** have a 'capital-gain' > 20,000 or 'capital-loss' > 5,000. A person with 'Income >50K' should rarely have zero hours per week.
    *   **Zero-Gain/Loss Constraints:** If 'Income' is <=50K, 'capital-gain' must be 0 with >90% probability. 'capital-loss' must be 0 with >95% probability.

4.  **Statistical Fidelity:** The joint distribution of your generated data must closely match the real data's joint distribution. Pay special attention to correlations that are not explicitly in the graph but arise from the chains (e.g., `age -> education -> income` means income should be correlated with age). Do not introduce new correlations that are not present in the original data's conditional probability tables.

By following this strict, algorithmic procedure, you will produce synthetic data that is causally, logically, and statistically identical to the real human demographics. </Task>"

Instruction: Based on the analysis of the provided prompt-score pairs, the goal is to further lower the score by improving the causal structure and task guidance. The current causal structures miss crucial connections that lead to implausible synthetic data, such as the direct influence of *age* on *hours-per-week* and *Income*, or the influence of *native-country* on *education* and *occupation*. Additionally, the existing structures sometimes include redundant or misordered edges (e.g., *marital-status* causing *race* is unrealistic).

The updated prompt below introduces a more comprehensive and logically sound causal DAG that better reflects real-world demographic relationships. It also tightens the task guidance to enforce stricter conditional dependencies, plausible thresholds, and cross-variable consistency checks.

---

**Updated Prompt:**

```
<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('age', 'Income'),
 ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'Income'),
 ('marital-status', 'relationship'),
 ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('occupation', 'Income'),
 ('relationship', 'sex'),
 ('hours-per-week', 'Income'),
 ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('capital-gain', 'capital-loss'),
 ('native-country', 'education'), ('native-country', 'occupation'),
 ('race', 'occupation')]
</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that dutifully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Ensure strict adherence to the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above: generate each variable conditioned on its direct causes, using realistic conditional distributions derived from the data.

Enforce the following logical and demographic constraints:

1. **Age**: The root cause. Must be generated first. Allowed values are integers from 17 to 90. Older ages increase the likelihood of higher education (up to a threshold), being married/divorced, higher Income, and working more hours (but with diminishing returns after age 65). Extremely young ages (e.g., 17) must never be paired with high education (e.g., Doctorate) or high Income.

2. **Education**: Directly determined by age and native-country. Values are categorical (e.g., Preschool, 1st-4th, ..., Doctorate). Must logically imply education-num (e.g., Preschool→1, Doctorate→16). Higher education increases likelihood of professional occupations, more hours per week, and high Income.

3. **Native-country**: Directly influences education distribution (e.g., US has more high-school graduates; other countries may skew lower) and occupation (e.g., farming/fishing more common in certain countries). Use realistic demographic patterns from the data.

4. **Race**: Directly influences occupation (e.g., certain races are overrepresented in managerial roles or underrepresented in skilled labor). Must not be influenced by marital-status or relationship. Use conditional distributions from the data.

5. **Marital-status**: Caused only by age. Must be consistent with relationship (e.g., 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' or 'Wife'; 'Divorced' → 'Not-in-family' or 'Unmarried'; 'Never-married' → 'Own-child' or 'Not-in-family'). Avoid impossible pairings (e.g., 'Never-married' with 'Husband').

6. **Workclass**: Caused by occupation (not sex). For example, 'Exec-managerial' often maps to 'Private' or 'Self-emp-inc'; 'Farming-fishing' maps to 'Self-emp-not-inc' or 'Private'. Ensure mutual exclusivity.

7. **Occupation**: Caused by age, education, native-country, and race. Must be plausible given age (e.g., no 'Exec-managerial' for age < 25) and education (e.g., no 'Prof-specialty' for less than high school). Use conditional distributions to avoid mismatches.

8. **Hours-per-week**: Caused by age, education, and occupation. Professionals often work 40-50 hours; part-time roles (e.g., 'Sales', 'Service') may be <30; farmers/fishers may work >50 but not 0. Hours must be an integer between 1 and 99. Avoid extremes for low-education occupations.

9. **Income**: Binary (<=50K or >50K). Caused by age, education, occupation, and hours-per-week. High Income must be rare for low education, low hours, or low-skill occupations. For example, a 20-year-old high-school dropout working 20 hours/week as 'Handlers-cleaners' must never have Income >50K.

10. **Capital-gain and capital-loss**: Both derived from Income. Capital-gain (0-99999) should only be non-zero for Income >50K (e.g., high-income professionals). Capital-loss (0-4356) must only be non-zero when capital-gain is non-zero (reflecting investment activity). Both should follow plausible distributions from the data.

11. **Sex**: Caused by relationship. 'Husband' implies male; 'Wife' implies female; 'Own-child' can be either. Must not causally influence any other variable (age, education, etc.). Ensure this is strictly enforced to avoid reverse causality.

**Validation**: After generating each row, check that all conditional dependencies are satisfied. Reject any row that violates the DAG, contains impossible age-education combinations, implausible marital-status/relationship pairs, or inconsistent Income-occupation linkages. The synthetic data must be indistinguishable from real human demographics by ensuring all causal pathways are realistically reflected, with no feature values violating real-world constraints. </Task>
```

   - Do not allow sex to cause age, education, or marital-status.  
   - Race is influenced only by marital-status (though typically independent; here we follow the given DAG strictly).

Here is the optimized prompt:

---
"<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'race'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'workclass'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('capital-gain', 'capital-loss'), ('workclass', 'occupation'), ('native-country', 'occupation')]
</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that dutifully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Ensure strict adherence to the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above. Your generation process must follow this deterministic sequence:

1.  **Marginal Root Nodes:** First, generate 'age' and 'native-country' from the marginal distributions observed in the data without conditioning on any other features.
2.  **Conditional Generation:** Generate each remaining variable strictly conditioned only on its direct causes as defined in the graph. For example:
    *   `education` must be conditioned on `age`.
    *   `marital-status` must be conditioned on `age`.
    *   `education-num` must be conditioned on `education`.
    *   `occupation` must be conditioned on `education`, `workclass`, and `native-country`.
    *   `relationship` must be conditioned on `marital-status`.
    *   `race` must be conditioned on `relationship`.
    *   `sex` must be conditioned on `relationship`.
    *   `workclass` must be conditioned on `sex`.
    *   `hours-per-week` must be conditioned on `occupation`.
    *   `Income` must be conditioned on `education` and `hours-per-week`.
    *   `capital-gain` must be conditioned on `Income`.
    *   `capital-loss` must be conditioned on `capital-gain`.

3.  **Rigorous Logical & Statistical Validation:** After generating each sample, perform a check for the following specific constraints. If a violation is found, **reject the sample and regenerate it** until all constraints are met.
    *   **Age vs. Education:** A person under 18 cannot have a 'Doctorate' or 'Prof-school'. A person under 14 cannot have 'Assoc-voc' or 'Bachelors'. A person under 6 cannot 'Some-college'.
    *   **Age vs. Marital Status:** A person under 16 cannot be 'Married-civ-spouse' or 'Divorced'. A person under 14 cannot be 'Separated'.
    *   **Marital Status vs. Relationship:** 'Married-civ-spouse' must pair only with 'Husband' or 'Wife'. 'Not-in-family' must pair with 'Unmarried', 'Divorced', 'Separated', or 'Widowed'. 'Own-child' must pair with 'Never-married'.
    *   **Sex vs. Workclass:** Gender roles in professions must be statistically grounded in the raw data. For example, if 'Female' in the data has a 95% probability of being 'Private' workclass, do not generate a 'Female' with 'Self-emp-inc' 30% of the time. Use the empirical conditional probabilities from the data.
    *   **Income Consistency:** A person with 'Income <=50K' **cannot** have a 'capital-gain' > 20,000 or 'capital-loss' > 5,000. A person with 'Income >50K' should rarely have zero hours per week.
    *   **Zero-Gain/Loss Constraints:** If 'Income' is <=50K, 'capital-gain' must be 0 with >90% probability. 'capital-loss' must be 0 with >95% probability.

4.  **Statistical Fidelity:** The joint distribution of your generated data must closely match the real data's joint distribution. Pay special attention to correlations that are not explicitly in the graph but arise from the chains (e.g., `age -> education -> income` means income should be correlated with age). Do not introduce new correlations that are not present in the original data's conditional probability tables.

By following this strict, algorithmic procedure, you will produce synthetic data that is causally, logically, and statistically identical to the real human demographics. </Task>""

Here is the optimized prompt, designed to push the score below 40% (and ideally into the 20-30% range) by adding a key causal connection, tightening conditional generation rules, and enforcing stricter scope constraints for rare events.

---

"<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'occupation'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('capital-gain', 'capital-loss'), ('workclass', 'occupation'), ('native-country', 'occupation'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'workclass')]
</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that dutifully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Ensure strict adherence to the directed acyclic graph (DAG) above. Your generation process must follow this rigorous, data-driven sequence:

1.  **Root Node Generation:** First, generate 'age', 'native-country', 'race', and 'sex' from their marginal distributions in the provided data. Do not condition these on any other variables.

2.  **Conditional Generation (with empirical probability constraints):** Generate each remaining variable strictly conditioned only on its direct causes as defined in the graph. For categorical variables, use the empirical conditional probabilities from the data (e.g., P(occupation | education, workclass, native-country, race)). For continuous variables, sample from realistic distributions conditioned on the parents. This means:
    *   `education` is conditioned on `age`.
    *   `marital-status` is conditioned on `age`.
    *   `occupation` is conditioned on `age`, `education`, `workclass`, `native-country`, and `race`.
    *   `workclass` is conditioned on `sex`.
    *   `education-num` is conditioned on `education` (use the exact mapping from the data).
    *   `relationship` is conditioned on `marital-status` (use the exact mapping from the data).
    *   `hours-per-week` is conditioned on `occupation` (e.g., 'Exec-managerial' has a mean of 45 hours, while 'Priv-house-serv' has a mean of 35; use these to sample).
    *   `Income` is conditioned on `education` and `hours-per-week` (e.g., high education + high hours = high probability of >50K).
    *   `capital-gain` is conditioned on `Income` (e.g., for <=50K, set to 0 with 96% probability; sample from non-zero values only for >50K, and never exceed $99,999).
    *   `capital-loss` is conditioned on `capital-gain` (e.g., if capital-gain is >0, capital-loss must be 0 in 95% of cases; otherwise, sample from low values <5000).

3.  **Rigorous Logical & Statistical Validation (Sample Rejection + Regeneration):** After generating each sample, perform these checks. If *any* violation is found, **reject the sample and regenerate it** until all constraints are met.
    *   **Age vs. Education:** No one under 14 can have 'Bachelors' or higher. No one under 18 can have 'Doctorate' or 'Prof-school'. No one over 100 can have 'Preschool'.
    *   **Age vs. Marital Status:** No one under 16 can be 'Married-civ-spouse' or 'Divorced'. No one under 18 can be 'Separated' or 'Widowed'.
    *   **Marital Status vs. Relationship:** 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' or 'Wife' only. 'Never-married' → 'Not-in-family' or 'Own-child' only. 'Divorced' → 'Not-in-family' or 'Unmarried' only.
    *   **Occupation Plausibility:** 'Farming-fishing' must have 'hours-per-week' between 20 and 80. 'Priv-house-serv' must be exclusively 'Female' (from data demographic). 'Armed-Forces' must have age between 18 and 45.
    *   **Income-Capital-Loss Link:** For 'Income >50K', 'capital-loss' must be <10,000 in 99% of cases. For 'Income <=50K', 'capital-loss' must be 0 in 98% of cases.
    *   **Sex-Workclass-Occupation Consistency:** If 'sex' is 'Male', 'workclass' 'Private' probability is 65% (from data). If 'sex' is 'Female', 'workclass' 'Private' probability is 85% (from data). Use exact empirical distributions.

4.  **Statistical Fidelity to the Real Data:** The joint distribution of your generated data must closely match the real data's joint distribution on all two-way and three-way interactions that are implied by the causal graph (e.g., the correlation between age, occupation, and income; the correlation between race, occupation, and workclass). Do not introduce new correlations (e.g., do not make native-country directly cause income if the graph says it only causes occupation).

By following this strict, algorithmic procedure with detailed conditional probability constraints, you will produce synthetic data that is causally sound, logically consistent, and statistically identical to real human demographics. </Task>""
